```text
143	
144	\paragraph{QwQ-32B (Qwen).} The results decompose the metric disagreement into two components. (A)~The crossing-rate shallow-layer preference is label-independent: 100\% of random-label repeats still select layers L0--L7, confirming that this bias reflects representation geometry (shallow-layer cosine distance compression; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}) rather than any property of the safety labels. The CR-composite inherits this bias (80\% shallow). (B)~Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR) select deep layers only with real safety labels; under random labels, selections scatter across the full layer range (mean L34--L36, high variance). The full metric disagreement (L4 vs.\ L63, a 59-layer gap) is the superposition of a geometric artifact on the crossing-rate side and a real safety signal on the threshold-based side.
145	
146	\paragraph{R1-8B (Llama).} The Llama-based model shows stronger label dependence: crossing rate scatters from L0 to mean L$10.7 \pm 4.8$ under random labels (0\% shallow), unlike QwQ-32B where it retains shallow preference. Threshold-based metrics degenerate entirely: precision and inverse FPR select L0 in all 10 repeats with zero variance, indicating random labels destroy discriminative signal rather than merely redistributing it. The real-CP disagreement (L0 vs.\ L23, a 23-layer gap) vanishes completely under random labels.
147	
148	\paragraph{Cross-architecture summary.} The metric disagreement requires genuine safety labels in both architectures, but the mechanism differs: in QwQ-32B, crossing-rate shallow preference reflects representation geometry (label-independent cosine compression), while in R1-8B even crossing-rate layer selection is label-dependent. The geometric component of the crossing-rate bias is thus Qwen-specific rather than a universal property of transformer representations.
149	
150	\section{Representation Compression Analysis}
151	\label{app:cosine_volatility}
152	
153	We measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).
154	
155	\begin{table}[htbp]
156	\centering
157	\small
158	\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}
159	\label{tab:cosine_volatility}
160	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
161	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
162	\toprule
163	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Layer (type)} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{Std} \\
164	\midrule
165	R1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\
166	 & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\[2pt]
167	OT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\
168	 & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\[2pt]
169	QwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\
170	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\[2pt]
171	R1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\
172	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\
173	\bottomrule
174	\end{tabular}}
175	\end{table}
176	
177	Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.
178	
179	\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}
180	To assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. The deep-layer mean cosine distance (0.442) is $1.67\times$ the shallow-layer mean (0.265), consistent with the 1.4--2.7$\times$ range observed across all four models on HarmThoughts (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}). This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.
181	
182	\subsection{AdvBench Boundary Condition}
183	\label{app:advbench_cp}
184	
185	We analyze R1-8B behavior on 388 substantive-response AdvBench~\citep{advbench} traces (of 520 total) to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).
186	
187	\begin{table}[htbp]
188	\centering
189	\small
190	\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (388 substantive-response traces of 520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}
191	\label{tab:advbench_boundary}
192	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
193	\begin{tabular}{lc}
194	\toprule
195	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Value} \\
196	\midrule
197	Jailbreak rate & 13.4\% \\
198	CP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\% \\
199	Mean CP position (among JB) & 1.4 steps \\
200	\bottomrule
201	\end{tabular}}
202	\end{table}
203	
204	Adversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.
205	
206	\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}
207	\label{app:holm_bonferroni}
208	
209	\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.
210	
211	\begin{table}[htbp]
212	\centering
213	\small
214	\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 tests (4 models $\times$ 3 correlated metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All 12 pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$; a 4-test model-level correction (one per model, using the worst $p$-value) also passes.}
215	\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}
216	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
217	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
218	\begin{tabular}{clllcc}
219	\toprule
220	\textbf{Rank} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Metric} & \textbf{$p$-val} & \textbf{Thresh.} & \textbf{Pass} \\
221	\midrule
222	1  & R1-8B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0042 & \checkmark \\
223	2  & OT-7B  & Bal.\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0045 & \checkmark \\
224	3  & OT-7B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0050 & \checkmark \\
225	4  & OT-7B  & FPR & $<$0.001 & 0.0056 & \checkmark \\
226	5  & QwQ-32B & Bal.\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0063 & \checkmark \\
227	6  & QwQ-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0071 & \checkmark \\
228	7  & R1-32B & Bal.\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0083 & \checkmark \\
229	8  & R1-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0100 & \checkmark \\
230	9  & QwQ-32B & FPR & 0.004 & 0.0125 & \checkmark \\
231	10 & R1-8B  & Bal.\ Acc. & 0.005 & 0.0167 & \checkmark \\
232	11 & R1-32B & FPR & 0.007 & 0.0250 & \checkmark \\
233	12 & R1-8B  & FPR & 0.044 & 0.0500 & \checkmark \\
234	\bottomrule
235	\end{tabular}}
236	\end{table}
237	
238	The weakest test (rank 12, R1-8B FPR, $p{=}0.044$) passes its threshold ($\alpha/1 = 0.05$) with margin. The ordering reveals that OT-7B produces the strongest HS-text separation (ranks 2--4), while R1-8B FPR is the least significant despite R1-8B's precision gap (+20.1pp at $W{=}3$). This reflects R1-8B's already-low text FPR (11.8\%), which leaves less room for HS improvement on this metric.
239	
240	\paragraph{Exact sign-flip permutation test validation.} As an independent check on bootstrap significance for the small-sample 32B models, we run exact sign-flip permutation tests on all six 32B comparisons, enumerating all $2^n$ sign permutations ($2^{19}{=}524{,}288$ for QwQ-32B, $2^{20}{=}1{,}048{,}576$ for R1-32B). All six tests reject the null at $\alpha{=}0.05$: QwQ-32B (bal\_acc $p{<}0.001$, precision $p{=}0.003$, FPR $p{<}0.001$); R1-32B (bal\_acc $p{=}0.008$, precision $p{<}0.001$, FPR $p{=}0.004$). The permutation $p$-values are consistent with the bootstrap values in \cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} and confirm that the conclusions are not artifacts of distributional assumptions at small $n$.
```

```json
      "content": "\\appendix\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Appendix Contents:}\n\\cref{app:checklist}~Responsible NLP Research Checklist;\n\\cref{app:layer_sweep}~Layer Sweep Results;\n\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}~Representation Compression Analysis;\n\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}~Holm-Bonferroni Correction;\n\\cref{app:window_ablation}~Window Size Ablation;\n\\cref{app:ot7b_details}~OpenThinker-7B Details;\n\\cref{app:gru_details}~GRU Training Details;\n\\cref{app:control}~Control Task Methodology;\n\\cref{sec:oracle}~Oracle Upper Bound;\n\\cref{app:cp_perturbation}~CP Annotation Perturbation;\n\\cref{app:bocpd_config}~BOCPD/CUSUM Configuration;\n\\cref{app:k_ablation}~Detection Threshold Ablation;\n\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}~Decision Threshold Sensitivity;\n\\cref{app:bug}~M002 Bug Disclosure;\n\\cref{app:two_stage}~Adaptive Two-Stage Detection;\n\\cref{app:temporal_details}~Temporal Ablation Details;\n\\cref{app:negative_details}~Negative Results Details (incl.\\ \\cref{tab:negative});\n\\cref{app:transfer_details}~Cross-Model Transfer Details;\n\\cref{app:encoder_robustness}~Encoder Robustness;\n\\cref{app:random_projection}~Random Projection Baseline;\n\\cref{app:e5_mistral}~LLM-Based Text Encoder;\n\\cref{app:position_residual}~Position-Residualized Probing;\n\\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}~Hyperparameter Robustness;\n\\cref{app:multi_seed}~Multi-Seed Stability;\n\\cref{app:split_half}~Split-Half Internal Validity;\n\\cref{app:ba_layer_comparison}~BA-Layer Modality Comparison;\n\\cref{app:power_analysis}~Post-Hoc Power Analysis.\n\n\\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\\label{app:checklist}\n\nWe address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\\cref{sec:limitations}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark. We acknowledge a dual-use concern: safety probes that detect commitment could in principle be used to craft reasoning traces that evade detection. We believe the defensive value of understanding commitment dynamics outweighs this risk, as evasion requires white-box access to the probe, which a deployed monitoring system would not expose.\n    \\item \\textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.\n    \\item \\textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations, and AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} for cross-dataset geometric validation. No new data collection was performed.\n    \\item \\textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \\cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\n\\section{Layer Sweep Results}\n\\label{app:layer_sweep}\n\nFor each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate (the CR-composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The CR-composite systematically selects shallow layers across all four models (including R1-8B under the full L0--L24 sweep; \\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. Three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers for all models; pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies with the data split (under 70/30: L30 for QwQ-32B, L47 for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20: L63 and L46 respectively; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: under the original L12--L24 sweep, all five metrics agreed on L14 (44\\%); extending to L0--L24, crossing rate shifts to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14, a 16-layer gap (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). OT-7B: CR-composite selects L0 (0\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L16 (57\\%). QwQ-32B: CR-composite selects L2 (3\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\\%). R1-32B: CR-composite selects L10 (16\\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\\%). The disagreement is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the CR-composite selects layers in the bottom 16\\% of the network while threshold-based metrics select layers at 98\\% depth. Under 70/30, pure balanced accuracy selects L30 (47\\%) for QwQ-32B and L47 (73\\%) for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20, it selects L63 (98\\%) and L46 (72\\%), which demonstrates sensitivity to the data split.\n\n\\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the CR-composite layers, HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the threshold-based deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection stability (LOO-CV).} Leave-one-out cross-validation on the validation set shows moderate consistency for 7--8B models (65--68\\% dominant-layer agreement) but lower for 32B, confirming that layer selection instability is particularly acute at small sample sizes. Under pure balanced accuracy, the dominant layer is selected in 68\\% (QwQ-32B: L30, 13/19 folds) and 65\\% (R1-32B: L47, 13/20 folds) of folds. Alternative selections scatter across neighboring layers (L29, L55--56 for QwQ; L32, L38, L43 for R1-32B), reinforcing the need for multi-metric auditing rather than single-metric layer selection. Threshold-based metrics show tighter convergence to L63 for both 32B models. Expanding the criterion from top-1 to top-3 layers does not change conclusions: the top-3 BA-selected layers for QwQ-32B (L30, L29, L55) and R1-32B (L47, L43, L38) all fall in the mid-to-deep range, and threshold-based metrics remain converged at L63 across all folds.\n\n\\paragraph{Joint (layer, $W$) sensitivity.} Under the original 70/30 validation protocol, we evaluate QwQ-32B across 64 layers $\\times$ 3 windows ($W \\in \\{1, 3, 5\\}$) (192 configurations) (\\cref{tab:joint_layer_w}). L60 is near-optimal: within 2.1pp of the global best at $W{=}3$ and the top-ranked layer at $W{=}5$. (Under 60/20/20, threshold-based metrics select L63; the shift from L60 to L63 across splits itself illustrates layer selection sensitivity.) The optimal layer shifts from middle-deep (L46--47 at $W{=}1$) to deep (L60 at $W{=}5$), confirming a layer-window interaction. For R1-8B, optimal precision layers shift similarly (L16 at $W{=}1$ $\\to$ L9 at $W{=}15$; Kendall's $\\tau{=}{-}0.91$, $p{=}0.071$), a consistent negative trend that does not reach significance at $n{=}4$ windows. L30, selected by per-fold LOO-CV at $W{=}1$ under balanced accuracy, is suboptimal across all windows (max BA $= 0.769$ vs.\\ global best 0.817). Joint (layer, $W$) stability was evaluated via grid search on $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5\\}$ for QwQ-32B only; full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{QwQ-32B joint (layer, $W$) grid search under 70/30 validation. L60 is near-optimal at recommended windows ($W{=}3$--$5$). L30 (LOO-CV selection at $W{=}1$) is suboptimal across all windows. Under the canonical 60/20/20 split, threshold-based metrics select L63.}\n\\label{tab:joint_layer_w}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{clccc}\n\\toprule\n$W$ & \\textbf{Best layer} & \\textbf{Best BA} & \\textbf{L60 BA} & \\textbf{L60 rank} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & L47 & 0.798 & 0.783 & $\\sim$15th \\\\\n3 & L46 & 0.817 & 0.796 & $\\sim$6th \\\\\n5 & L60 & 0.808 & 0.808 & 1st \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{R1-8B full-layer sweep.}\n\\label{app:r18b_full_layer}\nThe original R1-8B layer sweep covered L12--L24 (13 layers), where all five metrics agreed on L14 (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). Extending to L0--L24 reveals that crossing rate consistently selects L0 across all window sizes, while threshold-based metrics retain mid-to-deep layers (L12--L16 for $W{=}1$--$5$; L9 at $W{=}15$, marginal $\\Delta{=}0.003$ between shallow and deep means), a 9--16 layer divergence (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). Unlike 32B models, the bias is isolated to crossing rate; threshold-based metrics show no shallow shift for R1-8B. This confirms the crossing-rate bias generalizes to Llama-architecture models. HS probes maintain significant advantage across all 25 layers and window sizes (precision gap ${\\sim}$0.15--0.17), unlike OT-7B and 32B models where significance drops at shallow layers.\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B full-layer sweep (L0--L24, extending the original L12--L24 range). Best layer selected by crossing rate vs.\\ threshold-based metrics across four window sizes. Score = balanced accuracy at the threshold-based layer. Layer gap = crossing-rate layer minus threshold-based layer.}\n\\label{tab:r18b_full_layer}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& $W{=}1$ & $W{=}3$ & $W{=}5$ & $W{=}15$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCrossing rate & L0 & L0 & L0 & L0 \\\\\nThreshold (composite) & L16 (0.789) & L12 (0.796) & L12 (0.803) & L9 (0.784) \\\\\n\\midrule\nLayer gap & 16 & 12 & 12 & 9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Random-Label Decomposition Control}\n\\label{app:random_cp}\n\nTo decompose the sources of metric disagreement in layer selection, we replace the true CP annotations with uniformly random labels and re-run the full layer sweep (10 random-label repeats per model). We test QwQ-32B (95 traces, 64 layers) and R1-8B (209 traces, 25 layers); \\cref{tab:random_cp,tab:random_cp_r18b} report the fraction of repeats selecting a shallow layer and the mean selected layer.\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{QwQ-32B layer selection under random CP labels (10 repeats). Shallow\\%: fraction selecting a layer L$\\leq$10. Real-CP layer shown for reference.}\n\\label{tab:random_cp}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Real-CP} & \\textbf{Shallow\\%} & \\textbf{Rand.\\ mean layer} \\\\\n\\midrule\nCrossing rate & L4 & 100\\% & $3.4 \\pm 2.6$ \\\\\nCR-composite & L4 & 80\\% & $13.1 \\pm 18.1$ \\\\\nPrecision & L63 & 20\\% & $33.8 \\pm 19.8$ \\\\\nInv.\\ FPR & L62 & 10\\% & $36.4 \\pm 17.0$ \\\\\nBal.\\ accuracy & L63 & 20\\% & $33.3 \\pm 20.3$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B layer selection under random CP labels (10 repeats). Shallow\\%: fraction selecting L$\\leq$4 (bottom quintile of 25 layers). $\\dagger$: degenerate, all repeats select L0 with zero variance, indicating destroyed signal rather than shallow preference.}\n\\label{tab:random_cp_r18b}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Real-CP} & \\textbf{Shallow\\%} & \\textbf{Rand.\\ mean layer} \\\\\n\\midrule\nCrossing rate & L0 & 0\\% & $10.7 \\pm 4.8$ \\\\\nCR-composite & L0 & 0\\% & $13.7 \\pm 6.7$ \\\\\nPrecision & L23 & 100\\%$^{\\dagger}$ & $0.0 \\pm 0.0$ \\\\\nInv.\\ FPR & L13 & 100\\%$^{\\dagger}$ & $0.0 \\pm 0.0$ \\\\\nBal.\\ accuracy & L19 & 0\\% & $14.0 \\pm 4.0$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{QwQ-32B (Qwen).} The results decompose the metric disagreement into two components. (A)~The crossing-rate shallow-layer preference is label-independent: 100\\% of random-label repeats still select layers L0--L7, confirming that this bias reflects representation geometry (shallow-layer cosine distance compression; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}) rather than any property of the safety labels. The CR-composite inherits this bias (80\\% shallow). (B)~Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR) select deep layers only with real safety labels; under random labels, selections scatter across the full layer range (mean L34--L36, high variance). The full metric disagreement (L4 vs.\\ L63, a 59-layer gap) is the superposition of a geometric artifact on the crossing-rate side and a real safety signal on the threshold-based side.\n\n\\paragraph{R1-8B (Llama).} The Llama-based model shows stronger label dependence: crossing rate scatters from L0 to mean L$10.7 \\pm 4.8$ under random labels (0\\% shallow), unlike QwQ-32B where it retains shallow preference. Threshold-based metrics degenerate entirely: precision and inverse FPR select L0 in all 10 repeats with zero variance, indicating random labels destroy discriminative signal rather than merely redistributing it. The real-CP disagreement (L0 vs.\\ L23, a 23-layer gap) vanishes completely under random labels.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-architecture summary.} The metric disagreement requires genuine safety labels in both architectures, but the mechanism differs: in QwQ-32B, crossing-rate shallow preference reflects representation geometry (label-independent cosine compression), while in R1-8B even crossing-rate layer selection is label-dependent. The geometric component of the crossing-rate bias is thus Qwen-specific rather than a universal property of transformer representations.\n\n\\section{Representation Compression Analysis}\n\\label{app:cosine_volatility}\n\nWe measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}\n\\label{tab:cosine_volatility}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Layer (type)} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{Std} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\\\\n & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\\\[2pt]\nOT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\\\\n & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\\\[2pt]\nQwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\\\\n & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\\\[2pt]\nR1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\\\\n & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nShallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}\nTo assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. The deep-layer mean cosine distance (0.442) is $1.67\\times$ the shallow-layer mean (0.265), consistent with the 1.4--2.7$\\times$ range observed across all four models on HarmThoughts (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}). This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.\n\n\\subsection{AdvBench Boundary Condition}\n\\label{app:advbench_cp}\n\nWe analyze R1-8B behavior on 388 substantive-response AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} traces (of 520 total) to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (388 substantive-response traces of 520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}\n\\label{tab:advbench_boundary}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\nJailbreak rate & 13.4\\% \\\\\nCP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\\% \\\\\nMean CP position (among JB) & 1.4 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAdversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.\n\n\\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}\n\\label{app:holm_bonferroni}\n\n\\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \\alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 tests (4 models $\\times$ 3 correlated metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All 12 pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$; a 4-test model-level correction (one per model, using the worst $p$-value) also passes.}\n\\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{clllcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Rank} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{$p$-val} & \\textbf{Thresh.} & \\textbf{Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1  & R1-8B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0042 & \\checkmark \\\\\n2  & OT-7B  & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0045 & \\checkmark \\\\\n3  & OT-7B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0050 & \\checkmark \\\\\n4  & OT-7B  & FPR & $<$0.001 & 0.0056 & \\checkmark \\\\\n5  & QwQ-32B & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0063 & \\checkmark \\\\\n6  & QwQ-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0071 & \\checkmark \\\\\n7  & R1-32B & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0083 & \\checkmark \\\\\n8  & R1-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0100 & \\checkmark \\\\\n9  & QwQ-32B & FPR & 0.004 & 0.0125 & \\checkmark \\\\\n10 & R1-8B  & Bal.\\ Acc. & 0.005 & 0.0167 & \\checkmark \\\\\n11 & R1-32B & FPR & 0.007 & 0.0250 & \\checkmark \\\\\n12 & R1-8B  & FPR & 0.044 & 0.0500 & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe weakest test (rank 12, R1-8B FPR, $p{=}0.044$) passes its threshold ($\\alpha/1 = 0.05$) with margin. The ordering reveals that OT-7B produces the strongest HS-text separation (ranks 2--4), while R1-8B FPR is the least significant despite R1-8B's precision gap (+20.1pp at $W{=}3$). This reflects R1-8B's already-low text FPR (11.8\\%), which leaves less room for HS improvement on this metric.\n\n\\paragraph{Exact sign-flip permutation test validation.} As an independent check on bootstrap significance for the small-sample 32B models, we run exact sign-flip permutation tests on all six 32B comparisons, enumerating all $2^n$ sign permutations ($2^{19}{=}524{,}288$ for QwQ-32B, $2^{20}{=}1{,}048{,}576$ for R1-32B). All six tests reject the null at $\\alpha{=}0.05$: QwQ-32B (bal\\_acc $p{<}0.001$, precision $p{=}0.003$, FPR $p{<}0.001$); R1-32B (bal\\_acc $p{=}0.008$, precision $p{<}0.001$, FPR $p{=}0.004$). The permutation $p$-values are consistent with the bootstrap values in \\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} and confirm that the conclusions are not artifacts of distributional assumptions at small $n$.\n\n\n\\section{Window Size Ablation Details}\n\\label{app:window_ablation}\n\n\\cref{tab:window_full} reports the complete window size ablation across all models and seven window sizes. The main text (\\cref{sec:temporal}) presents R1-8B results; here we provide the full data. \\cref{fig:window_ablation} visualizes the monotonic improvement on R1-8B: precision rises from 69.3\\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\\% ($W{=}25$) while FPR drops from 9.65\\% to 4.8\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_temporal_improvement.pdf}\n\\caption{Temporal window effect on R1-8B HS probe (L14). Precision: 69.3\\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\\% ($W{=}25$); FPR: 9.65\\% to 4.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:window_ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[htbp]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Window size ablation ```text
```
```text
Web search results for query: "reasoning model safety probing hidden states layer selection 2025 2026 arxiv"

Links: [{"title":"Reasoning Models Know When They’re Right: Probing Hidden States for Self-Verification | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=O6I0Av7683"},{"title":"[2504.05419] Reasoning Models Know When They're Right: Probing Hidden States for Self-Verification","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05419"},{"title":"Reasoning Models Know When They're Right: Probing Hidden States for   Self-Verification","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.05419"},{"title":"A BERTology View of LLM Orchestrations: Token- and Layer-Selective Probes for Efficient Single-Pass Classification","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.13288"},{"title":"Contrastive Reasoning Alignment: Reinforcement Learning from Hidden Representations","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17305"},{"title":"Stop Before You Fail: Operational Capability Boundaries for Mitigating Unproductive Reasoning in Large Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.24711"},{"title":"Refusal Falls off a Cliff: How Safety Alignment Fails in Reasoning?","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.06036"},{"title":"Probing Hidden States for Calibrated, Alignment-Resistant Predictions in LLMs","url":"https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.09.17.25336018.full.pdf"}]

Based on the search results, I found several highly relevant papers from 2025 and 2026 on ArXiv about probing hidden states in reasoning models and safety:

## Key Papers Found:

1. **"Reasoning Models Know When They're Right: Probing Hidden States for Self-Verification"** (2025)
   - Studies whether reasoning models encode information about answer correctness through probing hidden states, with the resulting probe verifying intermediate answers with high accuracy and producing calibrated scores.
   - Uses the probe as a verifier to exit reasoning at intermediate answers during inference, reducing inference tokens by 24% without compromising performance.

2. **"A BERTology View of LLM Orchestrations: Token- and Layer-Selective Probes for Efficient Single-Pass Classification"** (2026)
   - Introduces an approach to search for signals across layer and token hidden states for safety applications.
   - Achieves safety goals using only 35M additional trainable parameters versus several billion for a separate guard model.

3. **"Contrastive Reasoning Alignment: Reinforcement Learning from Hidden Representations"** (2025)
   - Reveals clear geometric separation between safe and unsafe reasoning traces in hidden states from reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1.

4. **"Refusal Falls off a Cliff: How Safety Alignment Fails in Reasoning?"** (2025)
   - Uses probing-based methodology to quantify safety-relevant signals in hidden-state representations, training linear probe classifiers to predict refusal behavior from hidden states at different positions.

These papers collectively explore layer selection strategies, hidden state probing for safety verification, and interpretability of reasoning models in 2025-2026.
```

```json
      "Based on the search results, I found several highly relevant papers from 2025 and 2026 on ArXiv about probing hidden states in reasoning models and safety:\n\n## Key Papers Found:\n\n1. **\"Reasoning Models Know When They're Right: Probing Hidden States for Self-Verification\"** (2025)\n   - Studies whether reasoning models encode information about answer correctness through probing hidden states, with the resulting probe verifying intermediate answers with high accuracy and producing calibrated scores.\n   - Uses the probe as a verifier to exit reasoning at intermediate answers during inference, reducing inference tokens by 24% without compromising performance.\n\n2. **\"A BERTology View of LLM Orchestrations: Token- and Layer-Selective Probes for Efficient Single-Pass Classification\"** (2026)\n   - Introduces an approach to search for signals across layer and token hidden states for safety applications.\n   - Achieves safety goals using only 35M additional trainable parameters versus several billion for a separate guard model.\n\n3. **\"Contrastive Reasoning Alignment: Reinforcement Learning from Hidden Representations\"** (2025)\n   - Reveals clear geometric separation between safe and unsafe reasoning traces in hidden states from reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1.\n\n4. **\"Refusal Falls off a Cliff: How Safety Alignment Fails in Reasoning?\"** (2025)\n   - Uses probing-based methodology to quantify safety-relevant signals in hidden-state representations, training linear probe classifiers to predict refusal behavior from hidden states at different positions.\n\nThese papers collectively explore layer selection strategies, hidden state probing for safety verification, and interpretability of reasoning models in 2025-2026."
```

```text
Web search results for query: "early exit layer selection probing methodology sensitivity NLP 2025 2026"

Links: [{"title":"A Survey of Early Exit Deep Neural Networks in NLP","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.07670"},{"title":"Improving Prediction Certainty Estimation for Reliable Early Exiting via Null Space Projection","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.17249"},{"title":"A Simple Hash-Based Early Exiting Approach For Language Understanding   and Generation","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.01670"},{"title":"The Diminishing Returns of Early-Exit Decoding in Modern LLMs","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.23701"},{"title":"TIDE: Token-Informed Depth Execution for Per-Token Early Exit in LLM Inference","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.21365"}]

Based on the web search results, I found several relevant papers on early exit methods in NLP from 2025-2026:

**Recent Survey and Research:**

A comprehensive survey of early exit methods and their applications in NLP has been published, providing an overview of the field.

**Layer Selection and Exit Mechanisms:**

Each intermediate layer of pre-trained language models is paired with an internal classifier to provide an early prediction, and once the certainty level reaches a threshold, the forward inference is terminated, bypassing computation of deeper layers.

**Recent Methodological Approaches:**

Several recent papers address layer selection and sensitivity in early exiting:

1. **Null Space Projection Method**: Early exiting is an adaptive inference strategy recently proposed to accelerate the inference of pre-trained language models, with new approaches like improving prediction certainty estimation.

2. **Token-Informed Depth Execution (TIDE)**: This method trains lightweight binary classifiers on frozen model hidden states, using cosine similarity between checkpoint layers and the final layer as the convergence signal.

3. **Modern LLM Challenges**: Recent LLMs differ substantially from earlier models in architecture and training methodology, adopting improved pretraining recipes and architectural changes that can shift intermediate computation toward later layers.

These papers represent current research into early exit layer selection probing and sensitivity analysis in NLP for 2025-2026.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

Towards Monitoring Misaligned Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2507.12428v1"},{"title":"[2511.04312] Probing the Probes: Methods and Metrics for Concept Alignment","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04312"},{"title":"Probing the Probes: Methods and Metrics for Concept Alignment","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.04312"},{"title":"Can We Predict Alignment Before Models Finish Thinking? Towards Monitoring Misaligned Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.12428"},{"title":"Probing by Analogy: Decomposing Probes into Activations for Better Interpretability and Inter-Model Generalization | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=IhI1qGyk6S"}]

```text
Based on the search results, I found a highly relevant paper that matches your query:

"Can We Predict Alignment Before Models Finish Thinking? Towards Monitoring Misaligned Reasoning Models" by Chan et al. (2025) uses linear probes to detect alignment issues in reasoning models, showing that CoT texts are often unfaithful while model latents provide more reliable signals, and that alignment signals can be detected before reasoning completes.

The paper's findings suggest that lightweight probes could enable real-time safety monitoring and early intervention during generation, and generalize across model sizes, families, and safety benchmarks. The research also identifies a phenomenon called "performative CoTs" where reasoning consistently contradicts the final response as the chain-of-thought progresses.

The paper was published on arXiv on July 16, 2025 (arxiv.org/abs/2507.12428) and has a subsequent version from October 7, 2025.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```text
Links: [{"title":"HiddenDetect: Detecting Jailbreak Attacks against Large Vision-Language Models via Monitoring Hidden States","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2502.14744v1"},{"title":"Cloudfront","url":"https://www.techrxiv.org/doi/pdf/10.36227/techrxiv.176773228.86819800/v1"},{"title":"A Comprehensive Survey in LLM(-Agent) Full Stack Safety: Data, Training and Deployment","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2504.15585v1"},{"title":"Exposing the Ghost in the Transformer: Abnormal Detection for Large Language Models via Hidden State Forensics","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.00446"},{"title":"Probing Hidden States for Calibrated, Alignment-Resistant Predictions in LLMs","url":"https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.09.17.25336018v2.full.pdf"},{"title":"HiddenDetect: Detecting Jailbreak Attacks against Large Vision-Language Models via Monitoring Hidden States","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.14744"},{"title":"DeceptGuard :A Constitutional Oversight Framework For Detecting Deception in LLM Agents","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.13791"},{"title":"Probing Hidden States for Calibrated, Alignment-Resistant Predictions in LLMs","url":"https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.09.17.25336018.full.pdf"}]

I've found several relevant research papers and resources on hidden state monitoring and safety detection for LLMs. Here are the key findings:

## Recent Research (2025-2026)

**Hidden State Monitoring Approaches:**

Activation-based safety detection introduces minimal computational overhead and requires no additional model tuning, and unlike fine-tuned safety classifiers, generalizes to unseen adversarial prompts without requiring labeled training data.

Hidden-state filtering monitors and validates internal representations during processing to identify and neutralize adversarial content before influencing outputs, analyzing intermediate activations and attention patterns to detect anomalous processing indicating potential jailbreaks.

**Key Detection Methods:**

A novel approach to detecting abnormal behaviors in LLMs via hidden state forensics systematically inspects layer-specific activation patterns to develop a general framework that can efficiently identify a range of security threats in real-time without imposing prohibitive computational costs.

DECEPTGUARD is a unified framework that systematically compares three monitoring regimes: black-box monitors (actions and outputs only), CoT-aware monitors (additionally observing the agent's chain-of-thought reasoning trace), and activation-probe monitors (additionally reading hidden-state representations from a frozen open-weights encoder).

**Deployment Performance:**

Detection accuracies exceed 95% with consistently robust performance across multiple models in most scenarios, while the computational overhead remains minimal, with detector inference taking merely fractions of a second.

These recent works suggest that hidden state monitoring represents a promising direction for LLM safety during deployment, offering efficiency and generalization advantages over traditional behavioral-based approaches.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```json
            "url": "https://www.techrxiv.org/doi/pdf/10.36227/techrxiv.176773228.86819800/v1"
          },
          {
            "title": "A Comprehensive Survey in LLM(-Agent) Full Stack Safety: Data, Training and Deployment",
            "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2504.15585v1"
          },
          {
            "title": "Exposing the Ghost in the Transformer: Abnormal Detection for Large Language Models via Hidden State Forensics",
            "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.00446"
          },
          {
            "title": "Probing Hidden States for Calibrated, Alignment-Resistant Predictions in LLMs",
            "url": "https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.09.17.25336018v2.full.pdf"
          },
          {
            "title": "HiddenDetect: Detecting Jailbreak Attacks against Large Vision-Language Models via Monitoring Hidden States",
            "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.14744"
          },
          {
            "title": "DeceptGuard :A Constitutional Oversight Framework For Detecting Deception in LLM Agents",
            "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.13791"
          },
          {
            "title": "Probing Hidden States for Calibrated, Alignment-Resistant Predictions in LLMs",
            "url": "https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.09.17.25336018.full.pdf"
          }
        ]
      },
      "I've found several relevant research papers and resources on hidden state monitoring and safety detection for LLMs. Here are the key findings:\n\n## Recent Research (2025-2026)\n\n**Hidden State Monitoring Approaches:**\n\nActivation-based safety detection introduces minimal computational overhead and requires no additional model tuning, and unlike fine-tuned safety classifiers, generalizes to unseen adversarial prompts without requiring labeled training data.\n\nHidden-state filtering monitors and validates internal representations during processing to identify and neutralize adversarial content before influencing outputs, analyzing intermediate activations and attention patterns to detect anomalous processing indicating potential jailbreaks.\n\n**Key Detection Methods:**\n\nA novel approach to detecting abnormal behaviors in LLMs via hidden state forensics systematically inspects layer-specific activation patterns to develop a general framework that can efficiently identify a range of security threats in real-time without imposing prohibitive computational costs.\n\nDECEPTGUARD is a unified framework that systematically compares three monitoring regimes: black-box monitors (actions and outputs only), CoT-aware monitors (additionally observing the agent's chain-of-thought reasoning trace), and activation-probe monitors (additionally reading hidden-state representations from a frozen open-weights encoder).\n\n**Deployment Performance:**\n\nDetection accuracies exceed 95% with consistently robust performance across multiple models in most scenarios, while the computational overhead remains minimal, with detector inference taking merely fractions of a second.\n\nThese recent works suggest that hidden state monitoring represents a promising direction for LLM safety during deployment, offering efficiency and generalization advantages over traditional behavioral-based approaches."
    ],
    "durationSeconds": 7.358010925000009
  },
```

```text
Based on the web search results, here's what I found:

## HarmThoughts Dataset

HarmThoughts is a benchmark for analyzing safety at the level of reasoning processes rather than final outputs. Given a harmful prompt and a corresponding reasoning trace produced by jailbroken open-weight models, the task is to assign a behavioral label to each sentence based on its functional role in the progression toward (or away from) harmful compliance.

### Dataset Details

The dataset consists of 56,931 sentences from 1,018 reasoning traces generated by four model families, each annotated with fine-grained sentence-level behavioral labels. Each sentence is annotated using a taxonomy of 16 labels spanning three functional groups—harmful, harmless, and neutral—enabling fine-grained analysis of harm propagation in reasoning.

### Key Innovation

Unlike prior safety benchmarks that focus on whether an output is harmful, HarmThoughts captures how harm emerges through intermediate reasoning steps.

The research appears to address an important gap in AI safety by providing step-level annotation that can help understand how models reach unsafe conclusions through their reasoning processes, rather than just examining final outputs.
```

```text
Links: [{"title":"Layer by layer, module by module: Choose both for optimal OOD probing of ViT","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.05280"},{"title":"Noise Stability of Transformer Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.08287"},{"title":"Where meaning lives: Layer-wise accessibility of psycholinguistic features in encoder and decoder language models","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.03798"},{"title":"Investigating transformer models for textual bias detection in model, data, and dataspace cards | AI and Ethics | Springer Nature Link","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s43681-025-00975-3"},{"title":"A KL Lens on Quantization: Fast, Forward-Only Sensitivity for Mixed-Precision SSM–Transformer Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2604.13440v1"},{"title":"First is Not Really Better Than Last: Evaluating Layer Choice and Aggregation Strategies in Language Model Data Influence Estimation","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.04715"},{"title":"Why are Sensitive Functions Hard for Transformers?","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2402.09963"}]

I found several relevant research papers from 2025-2026 related to your search query. Here are the key findings:

**Layer Selection in Linear Probing:**

Research on pretrained Vision Transformers (ViTs) in out-of-distribution image classification tasks investigates when intermediate layers outperform the final layer in linear probing setups, identifying distribution shift between pretraining and downstream data as the driving factor.

Fine-grained analysis reveals that standard probing of transformer block outputs is suboptimal; probing the activation within the feedforward network yields the best performance under significant distribution shift, whereas the normalized output of the multi-head self-attention module is optimal when the shift is weak.

**Methodological Bias and Metric Sensitivity:**

Embedding extraction methods and model architecture affect both recoverable information and layer-wise localization, indicating that conclusions based on a single method may be unstable, and final-layer embeddings are rarely optimal for recovering psycholinguistic meaning with linear probes across architectures.

**Transformer Sensitivity Metrics:**

Theoretical work on Transformers has highlighted average sensitivity—the expected change in a model's output given a single-token perturbation—as a key metric. Despite its usefulness, average sensitivity has notable drawbacks: theoretically, its origins in Boolean analysis do not readily extend to real-valued domains, and empirically, it fails to fully explain the "junta-like" input dependence observed in models like GPT-2, GEMMA, and ROBERTA; researchers propose to instead quantify simplicity bias via noise stability.
```

```json
            "title": "Why are Sensitive Functions Hard for Transformers?",

      "I found several relevant research papers from 2025-2026 related to your search query. Here are the key findings:\n\n**Layer Selection in Linear Probing:**\n\nResearch on pretrained Vision Transformers (ViTs) in out-of-distribution image classification tasks investigates when intermediate layers outperform the final layer in linear probing setups, identifying distribution shift between pretraining and downstream data as the driving factor.\n\nFine-grained analysis reveals that standard probing of transformer block outputs is suboptimal; probing the activation within the feedforward network yields the best performance under significant distribution shift, whereas the normalized output of the multi-head self-attention module is optimal when the shift is weak.\n\n**Methodological Bias and Metric Sensitivity:**\n\nEmbedding extraction methods and model architecture affect both recoverable information and layer-wise localization, indicating that conclusions based on a single method may be unstable, and final-layer embeddings are rarely optimal for recovering psycholinguistic meaning with linear probes across architectures.\n\n**Transformer Sensitivity Metrics:**\n\nTheoretical work on Transformers has highlighted average sensitivity—the expected change in a model's output given a single-token perturbation—as a key metric. Despite its usefulness, average sensitivity has notable drawbacks: theoretically, its origins in Boolean analysis do not readily extend to real-valued domains, and empirically, it fails to fully explain the \"junta-like\" input dependence observed in models like GPT-2, GEMMA, and ROBERTA; researchers propose to instead quantify simplicity bias via noise stability."
    ],
```

```text
I now have a very comprehensive understanding of the paper. Let me summarize what I've found:

**Reference verification summary:**
- 82 bib entries, 82 cited, 0 orphan, 0 missing
- 61 verified online, 16 not found (likely because OpenAlex doesn't index recent arXiv preprints well), 5 mismatches (mostly author name encoding issues)
- The "not found" items are mostly well-known papers (DeepSeek-R1, QwQ, Turpin et al., etc.) that are genuine but not well-indexed in OpenAlex


- No fabricated references detected

Now I'm thinking through the novelty and completeness of the work. The title frames this as a study of layer selection sensitivity in safety probes, but the real contribution is identifying a methodological confound where the choice of evaluation metric can actually reverse the conclusions of safety probing studies. While prior work like Belinkov (2022) has touched on metric sensitivity, this paper's specific focus on how metric choice creates layer selection bias in the context of reasoning model safety appears to be the novel angle.

The Commitment Point concept is presented as a new contribution, but it's really a simplification of HarmThoughts' existing step-level annotation scheme—collapsing their 16-label taxonomy into a binary pre/post distinction. The "doubly conditional" framing is where things get interesting, though I need to see how that's developed to assess whether it's a genuine methodological advance or just a reframing of existing ideas.

The comparison to Chan et al. (2025) is handled reasonably—this work extends their single-layer, single-metric approach to multi-metric layer sensitivity analysis. The related work is comprehensive with 82 references covering reasoning safety and probing methodology, though there are a few gaps like the contrastive reasoning alignment work and some recent papers on layer selection for influence analysis.

The writing is dense but well-structured, with the abstract and introduction clearly laying out the problem and contribution. The "doubly conditional" framing captures the core insight about encoder quality and layer selection dependencies. The paper is heavily hedged with evidence tiers throughout—good scientific practice but it does undermine the findings somewhat. The title accurately reflects the main contribution, and while there are some markers of AI-assisted writing in the hedging patterns, the academic English is solid overall. The limitations section addresses key weaknesses like the single-dataset constraint and the 32B model scope.

The 22-page appendix is extensive and contains important ablations and ensemble analyses that arguably belong in the main text, though the space constraints are understandable given the number of models tested. The paper lacks computational cost analysis and real-world deployment evaluation. For EMNLP, this is well-positioned given the empirical methods focus and the broadly applicable methodological contribution about layer selection bias in probing studies. The critical issue is the heavy reliance on HarmThoughts for CP-dependent claims, with AdvBench only testing geometric properties.

The model set is Qwen-dominated (3 of 4 models), making the cross-architecture claims rest primarily on a single Llama model. The 32B samples are small (n=19-20), yet several key findings about position mediation and 32B divergence depend on them. The core methodological insight about different metrics selecting different layers may feel self-evident to researchers familiar with Belinkov (2022) and layer-wise information gradients, though the safety application does add practical stakes. The "Safety Commitment Point" concept is essentially a binarization of HarmThoughts' existing framework.

On the positive side, the experimental design is thorough with solid ablations and controls, the paper is admirably transparent about evidence tiers and limitations, and it delivers an important cautionary message for the probing community. The multi-metric layer selection taxonomy and comprehensive related work are also valuable contributions.
```

_source line 155 · 2026-05-21T19:05:29.685Z_

引用验证完成（82 条引用全部对应 bib 条目，无 orphan/missing；16 条 not_found 主要是 OpenAlex 未索引的新 arXiv 预印本，无伪造引用嫌疑）。现在综合所有信息撰写完整审稿意见。

---

## 总体评分
**Overall**: 3.5 (Borderline Conference)
**一句话总结**: 论文对 probing 领域 layer selection 偏差在 safety 场景下的放大效应提供了系统性实证，方法论贡献扎实但增量性质明显，核心发现的新颖性和泛化性受制于单一数据集和 Qwen 主导的模型集合。

## 官方维度打分（来自 ACL ARR Review Form）

| 维度 | 分数 | 理由摘要 |
|------|------|----------|
| Soundness | 3.5/5 | 统计方法严谨（nested CV, HB 校正, 置换检验），但 32B 结论受限于 n≤20 样本，且核心 finding（layer selection 可逆转结论）的泛化性仅在一个数据集上验证 |
| Excitement | 2.5/5 | 方法论警示有价值但属"已知问题在新领域的验证"，对 NLP 社区的直接实践指导有限 |
| Overall Assessment | 3.5/5 | Borderline Conference — 方法论贡献扎实但增量，可被接收为 Findings |
| Reproducibility | 4/5 | 超参数报告充分，附录详尽，承诺代码公开，计算成本低（<4 GPU-hours） |
| Confidence | 4/5 | 熟悉 probing methodology 和 LLM safety 领域 |

## Must-Fix 问题（影响接收决定）

**N1** [critical] Contribution 1（layer selection bias）的新颖性存在争议——本质是已知问题的领域迁移
- 具体位置：Section 1 contributions list (item 1), Section 2 "Probing Methodology and Layer Selection"
- 问题：Belinkov (2022) 已系统讨论了 probing metric sensitivity；Tenney et al. (2019) 和 Jawahar et al. (2019) 已确立 layer-wise information gradient。论文自己也承认 "Prior work has noted metric sensitivity in probing (Belinkov, 2022)"（Introduction, contribution 1）。核心发现——不同 metric 选择不同 layer 从而产生不同结论——是这些已知现象在 safety probing 场景下的自然推论。crossing-rate composite 选浅层是因为浅层 cosine compression 导致频繁 threshold crossing，这一机制是可预测的而非出人意料的。
- 为什么影响接收：ARR reviewer guidelines 明确要求评估 novelty，"state-of-the-art results are neither necessary nor sufficient for a scientific contribution"，但反过来，将已知方法论问题在新领域复现也不构成充分的新贡献。论文需要更清晰地论证：为什么 safety 领域的 qualitative reversal 不只是 quantitative 差异在高 stakes 下的放大，而是揭示了 probing 方法论中此前未被识别的结构性问题。
- 建议修改方向：(1) 加强论证为什么 safety 场景的 reversal 具有结构性差异（例如 safety signal 的空间分布与 linguistic probing 中的信息有什么根本不同）；(2) 在非 safety 的 probing 任务上做对比实验，证明 reversal 的严重程度确实在 safety domain 更大；(3) 考虑将 contribution 排序调整，将 "doubly conditional HS advantage" 的 empirical findings 提前。

**N2** [critical] 单一数据集（HarmThoughts）限制了所有 CP-dependent claims 的可信度
- 具体位置：Section 3.1, Section 4.1 (Setup), Limitations ¶1
- 问题：HarmThoughts 是唯一提供 step-level CP annotation 的公开数据集，但论文从这一个数据集上得出了跨模型、跨架构的结论。AdvBench 分析（Appendix B.2）仅验证了几何属性（cosine compression ratio），没有验证 probe performance 或 layer selection bias。论文的 sample sizes 已经很小（632 traces total, 95-228 per model），且 60/20/20 split 进一步压缩了 test set。
- 为什么影响接收：单数据集的结果无法排除数据集特异性的混淆。HarmThoughts 的 annotation scheme（16 labels binarized to pre/post CP）可能引入系统性偏差，且论文承认 "annotations lacking formal inter-annotator agreement"（Limitations ¶1），LLM-based proxy 的 κ=0.606 只达到 "moderate agreement"。
- 建议修改方向：(1) 在 AdvBench 上完成完整的 probe training + layer selection 实验（即使只对 R1-8B，因为 90.4% 的 jailbroken traces 有 CP=0，可以用非 jailbroken 的 traces）；(2) 至少说明为什么其他数据集（如引用的 Wang et al. 2025 survey 中提到的数据集）无法使用。

## Should-Fix 问题（影响评分但不一票否决）

**W1** [major] Safety Commitment Point 概念的原创性不足
- 具体位置：Section 3.1
- 问题：CP 定义为 "the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases"。HarmThoughts 已提供 step-level behavioral annotations，CP 本质上是将 HarmThoughts 的 16-label taxonomy 二值化为 pre/post commitment。论文对此只用一句 "This binary formulation is a deliberate simplification; we discuss its limits in Section 7" 带过。这不是一个独立的概念贡献，而是对已有标注的简单重编码。
- 建议：(1) 明确说明 CP 是 HarmThoughts annotation 的衍生定义而非独立贡献；(2) 讨论 binary CP 可能丢失的信息（例如 oscillating safety deliberation 在 binarization 后的信息损失）。

**W2** [major] 模型集合的架构多样性不足（3/4 Qwen）
- 具体位置：Table 1, Section 4.1
- 问题：4 个模型中 3 个是 Qwen 架构（OT-7B/QwQ-32B/R1-32B），仅 R1-8B 是 Llama。论文的 cross-architecture claims（如 "Contribution 1 generalizes across all four models (3 Qwen, 1 Llama)"，Discussion）本质上只有 n=1 的跨架构证据。且论文自己也发现 crossing-rate 的 geometric bias "is Qwen-specific"（Appendix B.1），这进一步削弱了泛化性声明。
- 建议：(1) 增加至少一个非 Qwen、非 Llama 架构的模型（如 Mistral-based reasoning model）；(2) 如果无法增加实验，应将泛化性声明的措辞显著弱化，明确表述为 "primarily Qwen-family results with limited cross-architecture evidence"。

**W3** [major] 32B 结果的证据力度无法支撑论文中的讨论篇幅
- 具体位置：Section 4.4, Table 4, Section 5
- 问题：32B 模型的 test set 仅 n=19-20，MDE at 80% power 为 8.6-9.5pp。论文用 Section 4.4 整个小节讨论 32B divergence，Discussion 中也大量引用 32B 结果。然而这些都标记为 exploratory（†），且 R1-32B 只在 W=1（25% coverage）下 significant。论文在 abstract 中也引用 32B 数据（"+15 to +19pp at 32B (exploratory†, n≤20)"），给读者造成了这些是稳健结论的印象。
- 建议：(1) 将 32B 分析移至附录或大幅缩减在正文中的篇幅；(2) 在 abstract 中不引用具体 32B 数字，改为 "preliminary evidence at 32B scale"。

**W4** [minor-major] "Doubly conditional" framing 可能过度包装了一个直觉性结论
- 具体位置：Abstract, Section 4.3
- 问题："The HS advantage is doubly conditional on encoder quality and position overlap" 作为 framing 听起来是一个深刻的发现，但其含义是：(1) 更强的 text encoder 缩小了 HS-text gap——这是 trivially expected；(2) 部分 HS advantage 可以用 temporal position 解释——这也是 probing 研究中常见的 confound。将两个 expected observations 组合起来命名为 "doubly conditional" 可能给读者误导认为这是一个 non-obvious finding。
- 建议：使用更中性的表述，直接说明 two confounds 而非创造术语。

## 小问题（不影响接收）

- **Title**: "Layer Selection Sensitivity" 准确但可能过于狭窄。论文实际上还包括 HS vs text comparison、temporal analysis、negative results 等，考虑标题是否需要更包容。
- **Abstract 密度**: Abstract 试图在一段中塞入所有数字结果（precision gains, p-values, layer divergence, 32B exploratory results），导致可读性差。建议简化数字，保留 narrative。
- **Contribution 排序**: Contribution 3（negative results）放在最后，但 seven alternative methods 全部失败本身是一个有价值的 finding，可能值得更高优先级。
- **引用密度**: Related Work (Section 2) 引用了 82 篇参考文献中的绝大部分，段落极密，建议适当精简以提高可读性。某些引用（如 SafeDream, HiddenDetect, SafeInt, RCS 等）只是列举式 one-sentence mention，对论文论证无实质支撑。
- **Table 2 readability**: Table 2 的 caption 使用了 4 个脚注标记（a, b, c, †），加上表格本身的数据，认知负担较高。
- **Appendix 体量**: 22 页附录包含重要内容（ensemble, AUROC analysis, power analysis），但部分内容（如 multi-metric ensemble, Table 42）本身就是 substantial findings，推到附录降低了可见性。

## Missing References（可能遗漏的相关工作）

1. **"Contrastive Reasoning Alignment: Reinforcement Learning from Hidden Representations"** (arXiv 2603.17305, 2025) — 直接研究 reasoning model hidden states 中 safe/unsafe traces 的几何分离，与本文的 representation analysis 高度相关。
2. **"Stop Before You Fail: Operational Capability Boundaries for Mitigating Unproductive Reasoning in Large Reasoning Models"** (arXiv 2509.24711) — 讨论 reasoning model 的 early stopping，与 layer selection/early exit 线索相关。
3. **"First is Not Really Better Than Last: Evaluating Layer Choice and Aggregation Strategies"** (arXiv 2511.04715) — 直接研究 layer choice 对 probing 结论的影响，虽然是 data influence 领域但方法论高度相关。
4. **"Exposing the Ghost in the Transformer: Abnormal Detection for LLMs via Hidden State Forensics"** (arXiv 2504.00446) — 层级 activation pattern 分析的 safety 检测框架。

## 主要优势

1. **方法论严谨性突出**: nested CV、Holm-Bonferroni 校正、置换检验、三层证据分级（cross-validated / significant / exploratory†）、random-label controls、control task selectivity——统计处理远超同领域平均水平。
2. **Transparency 标杆**: 论文对自身局限性的披露异常充分：32B 的 sample limitations、false positive rates 11-50%、encoder-conditional advantage、position mediation confound——几乎没有 overclaim。
3. **Negative results 的完整记录**: Table 24 记录了 7 种 alternative methods 的全面失败，附录提供了每种方法的详细配置和失败原因分析，这对社区有实际参考价值。
4. **Layer selection taxonomy 有实用性**: 5 种 metric 的分类（crossing-rate vs threshold-based）以及 multi-metric audit 建议（Appendix Z）为后续 probing 研究提供了可操作的检查清单。
5. **Reproducibility**: <4 GPU-hours 的低计算需求、详尽的超参数报告、承诺代码公开，大大降低了复现门槛。

## Questions（需要作者回答）

1. 如果在 HarmThoughts 之外的第二个 step-annotated 数据集上重复实验（即使是合成数据集），layer selection bias 是否仍然存在？作者是否尝试过从 AdvBench 构建一个小规模的 CP-annotated test set？
2. Crossing-rate composite 在本文中被作为 "straw man" 使用——是否有任何先前工作实际采用了 crossing rate 或类似指标来选择 probing layer？如果没有，那么 Contribution 1 的实际影响（correcting a mistake no one has made yet）可能需要重新定位。
3. Multi-metric ensemble（Appendix Z, Table 42）似乎完全消除了 layer selection bias 问题（p≤0.002），为什么不将其提升为主要方法贡献而非附录内容？
4. 为什么不测试 DeepSeek-R1 (671B) 或其他非 Qwen/Llama 架构的 reasoning model？如果是资源限制，这应该在 Limitations 中明确说明。

```json
  "text": "## Reviewer 2 审稿意见（新颖性与完整性）\n\n### 总体评分\n**Overall**: 3.5 (Borderline Conference)\n**一句话总结**: 论文对 probing 领域 layer selection 偏差在 safety 场景下的放大效应提供了系统性实证，方法论贡献扎实但增量性质明显，核心发现的新颖性和泛化性受制于单一数据集和 Qwen 主导的模型集合。\n\n### 官方维度打分（ACL ARR Review Form）\n\n| 维度 | 分数 | 理由摘要 |\n|------|------|----------|\n| Soundness | 3.5/5 | 统计方法严谨（nested CV, HB 校正, 置换检验），但 32B 结论受限于 n≤20 样本，单数据集限制泛化性 |\n| Excitement | 2.5/5 | 方法论警示有价值但属\"已知问题在新领域的验证\"，对 NLP 社区的直接实践指导有限 |\n| Overall Assessment | 3.5/5 | Borderline Conference — 方法论贡献扎实但增量，可被接收为 Findings |\n| Reproducibility | 4/5 | 超参数报告充分，附录详尽，承诺代码公开，<4 GPU-hours |\n| Confidence | 4/5 | 熟悉 probing methodology 和 LLM safety 领域 |\n\n---\n\n### Summary (2-3 句)\n\n本文研究了 safety probing 中 layer selection metric 的选择如何影响 hidden-state vs text probe 比较的结论。核心发现是 crossing-rate composite 系统性地选择浅层（导致 HS advantage 消失），而 threshold-based metrics 选择深层（恢复 HS advantage），同一数据上可产生 p=0.83 vs p<0.005 的定性逆转。论文在 4 个 reasoning model 上进行了系统实验，统计处理严谨，但新颖性受制于这本质上是已知 probing sensitivity 问题在 safety 领域的验证。\n\n---\n\n### Strengths\n\n1. **统计方法论标杆级别**: nested CV、Holm-Bonferroni step-down、exact sign-flip 置换检验、三层证据分级（cross-validated / significant / exploratory†）、random-label controls、control task selectivity——统计处理远超同领域平均水平。\n\n2. **Transparency 异常突出**: 32B sample limitations（n≤20 标记为 †）、false positive rates 11-50%、encoder-conditional advantage、position mediation confound——几乎没有 overclaim。\n\n3. **Negative results 完整记录有实际价值**: 7 种 alternative methods（BOCPD, CUSUM, Mahalanobis, RepE, MLP, CCS, cross-model transfer）全部失败，附录提供详细配置和失败原因分析（Table 24），对社区有参考价值。\n\n4. **Layer selection taxonomy 可操作**: 5 种 metric 分类（crossing-rate vs threshold-based）及 multi-metric audit 建议为后续 probing 研究提供了检查清单。\n\n5. **低复现门槛**: <4 GPU-hours、详尽超参数报告、承诺代码公开。\n\n---\n\n### Weaknesses\n\n**N1 [critical] Contribution 1 新颖性争议——已知问题的领域迁移**\n\nBelinkov (2022) 已系统讨论 probing metric sensitivity；Tenney et al. (2019) 和 Jawahar et al. (2019) 已确立 layer-wise information gradient。论文自承 \"Prior work has noted metric sensitivity in probing\"（Introduction）。crossing-rate composite 选浅层因浅层 cosine compression 导致频繁 threshold crossing，这一机制是可预测的。论文需论证 safety 领域的 qualitative reversal 为什么不只是已知问题在高 stakes 下的放大，而是揭示了此前未被识别的结构性问题。\n\n建议：(1) 在非 safety probing 任务上做对比，证明 reversal 严重程度在 safety domain 确实更大；(2) 分析 safety signal 空间分布与 linguistic probing 的根本差异；(3) 考虑调整 contribution 排序。\n\n**N2 [critical] 单一数据集限制所有 CP-dependent claims**\n\n全部 CP-dependent claims 依赖 HarmThoughts，且 annotations 缺乏 formal IAA（LLM proxy κ=0.606 仅 moderate agreement）。AdvBench 分析仅验证几何属性，未验证 probe performance。\n\n建议：(1) 在 AdvBench 上完成 probe training + layer selection 实验（即使 subset）；(2) 说明其他数据集无法使用的原因。\n\n**W1 [major] Safety Commitment Point 原创性不足**\n\nCP 本质上是将 HarmThoughts 16-label taxonomy 二值化。应明确说明 CP 是已有标注的衍生定义，讨论 binary formulation 的信息损失。\n\n**W2 [major] 架构多样性不足（3/4 Qwen, n=1 跨架构）**\n\ncrossing-rate geometric bias 已被发现是 Qwen-specific（Appendix B.1），进一步削弱泛化性声明。建议增加非 Qwen/Llama 模型或显著弱化泛化性措辞。\n\n**W3 [major] 32B exploratory 结果占正文篇幅过多**\n\nn=19-20 的 exploratory 结果在 Section 4.4 和 Discussion 中被大量讨论，abstract 引用具体 32B 数字（\"+15 to +19pp\"）可能误导读者。建议缩减正文篇幅，abstract 改用 \"preliminary evidence at 32B scale\"。\n\n**W4 [minor-major] \"Doubly conditional\" framing 过度包装**\n\n\"HS advantage doubly conditional on encoder quality and position overlap\" 本质上是两个 expected observations（更强 encoder 缩小 gap + temporal position 是常见 confound）的组合命名。建议使用更中性表述。\n\n---\n\n### Questions\n\n1. 是否尝试过在 AdvBench 或合成数据集上验证 layer selection bias？\n2. [Round 1 审稿请求]:
```
以下是 Reviewer 1（方法严谨性）的完整审稿意见。请逐条回应：对每个 Weakness/Question 标注 [AGREE] 同意并说明理由，或 [REBUT] 反驳并提供证据（cite 具体 Section/Table/Figure）。

---

## Reviewer 1 完整审稿意见

### Overall: 3.5 (Borderline Conference)

**一句话总结**: 该论文提出了一个重要且被低估的方法论问题——评估指标决定层选择进而决定结论，统计框架总体审慎，但 32B 模型的小样本推断可靠性、GRU 与 LR 实验数据划分不一致、以及有效自由度估计不足是需要解决的关键问题。

### Strengths
1. 重要且被低估的研究问题
2. 审慎的统计框架（三级证据梯度）
3. 极其全面的消融实验（26 个附录 section）
4. 透明的 bug 披露（Section N）
5. 校准良好的 claims
6. 强大的控制任务设计

### Must-Fix (Critical)

**N1 [critical] BCa bootstrap 在 32B 样本量（n≤20）下可能反保守**
BCa 校正需要从 jackknife 统计量估计 â 和 ẑ₀，19-20 个 jackknife 副本时估计值噪声大。统计文献指出 BCa 在 n<25 时可能反保守（CI 偏窄）。虽然 sign-flip permutation test 作为补充验证（6 项 32B 比较全部通过），但 bootstrap CI 仍是主要推断工具。
建议：(1) 同时报告 percentile bootstrap CI 和精确 permutation p-value；(2) 明确讨论 BCa 小样本局限性；(3) BC（非加速）bootstrap 作 sensitivity check。

**N2 [critical] 有效自由度估计可能严重不足**
层选择步骤在验证集上搜索了 28-64 层 × 7 窗口 = 196-448 个候选配置。论文 Section W 给出三个论据，但 32B 模型缺乏 nested 结构（仅 LOO-CV），未对自适应选择提供正式调整。且随机标签控制显示 crossing-rate 在随机标签下 100% 选浅层，暗示指标构造的内在偏向。
建议：(1) 32B 提供 nested LOO 或 split-conformal 框架；(2) 更透明讨论搜索空间 vs 最终检验数的落差；(3) 区分 "crossing-rate 选浅层是真实现象" vs "指标有内建偏向"。

### Should-Fix (Major)

**W1 [major] GRU 实验使用不兼容的数据划分**
GRU 用 70/30 split，LR 用 60/20/20，评估在不同测试集上。虽定位为 supplementary，但 Table 16 和 Discussion 直接比较了两者。
建议：用相同 60/20/20 split 重跑 GRU。

**W2 [major] "Doubly conditional" 表述可能夸大条件独立性**
metric 决定 layer（因果链），两者不是独立条件。"doubly conditional" 在 abstract 中可能误导。
建议：改为 "conditional on metric-induced layer selection" 或明确因果关联。

**W3 [major] Crossing-rate 的浅层偏好可能是指标构造的伪影**
随机标签控制显示 crossing-rate 在随机标签下 100% 选浅层。Contribution 1 未充分区分 "指标捕捉了真实现象" vs "指标有内建的浅层偏向"。

### Minor

**W4 [minor]** C=1.0 默认值缺乏原则性理由
**W5 [minor]** AUROC 排除削弱指标覆盖面
**W6 [minor]** Post-hoc power 分析与 R1-32B 结果的张力（残差化 gap +1.9pp 远低于 MDE ~9pp）
**W7 [minor]** 标注者间 CP 位置一致性仅中等（κ=0.606, 精确匹配 37%, MAD 8.5 步）

### Questions
Q1: 32B BCa CI vs percentile bootstrap CI vs permutation-based CI 对比？
Q2: GRU 能否在 60/20/20 split 下提供结果？
Q3: 随机标签下 crossing-rate 100% 选浅层，是否表明这是几何伪影？如何影响 C1？
Q4: R1-32B 残差化 gap +1.9pp 远低于 MDE ~9pp，能否说 R1-32B 位置校正后未检测到 HS 优势？
Q5: "doubly conditional" 是因果独立还是单一因果链？
Q6: CP 位置扰动 ±3 步的敏感度如何？（论文是否有 CP perturbation 分析？）

### Missing References
- Carpenter & Bithell (2000) — BCa 小样本性质

---
